- Sky Burial Peak: High windswept summit where bodies are offered to vultures, with prayer flags and thin air. Bones lie scattered on bare rock, flags snap in the cold wind, and oxygen is scarce. Players climb past exhaustion and frostbite to reach monks, perform a funeral rite, or retrieve a relic from the stone platform.
- Battlefield Graveyard: Scarred plain of broken weapons, shallow graves, and wandering ghosts of fallen soldiers. Rusted blades jut from churned earth, tattered banners flutter, and spectral drums sound at dusk. Players scavenge for old arms and armor, free trapped soldier spirits, or relive the final battle through haunting visions.
- Bog Burial Moor: Misty peat wetland preserving ancient sacrifices and corpses, with sucking mud and will-o-wisp lights. Pale, leathery bodies lie just beneath the surface, and the ground can swallow a careless traveler. Players follow drifting lights along hidden paths, uncover sacrificial offerings, and risk drowning or drawing the attention of bog spirits.
- Reliquary Vault: Guarded chamber of sacred bones and holy objects, protected by wards, relic keepers, and curses. Gilded caskets glow in candlelight behind iron grates, and faint prayers hang in the incense-laden air. Players plan a heist or a pilgrimage, bypass sacred wards and guardians, and decide whether a stolen relic brings blessing or ruin.
- Sarcophagus Chamber: Inner tomb room holding a stone coffin, with final traps, inscriptions, and the awakening occupant. The room is hushed and heavy, with carved lids, grave offerings, and warnings etched into every surface. Players reach the climax of a tomb delve here, face a boss who stirs from rest, and choose between treasure and escape.
## Forest & Woodland (temperate and cold)
### Temperate
- Deciduous Forest: Broadleaf woods that blaze in autumn and open in winter, rich with deer, foragers, and game. Seasons change the whole feel, from leafy shade in summer to bare branches and crunching leaves later. Players hunt, gather nuts and herbs, meet woodcutters, and watch for bandits who exploit the sheltering canopy.
- Mixed Forest: Blend of conifers and broadleaf trees offering varied cover, wildlife, and balanced lumber resources. Evergreen needles and shifting leaves create dappled light and diverse habitats along every trail. Players find easy travel, abundant game, and good timber, with foresters' camps, hidden shrines, and encounters that change from one stand to the next.
- Beech Wood: Smooth-barked tall trees casting deep shade over an open, leaf-carpeted floor, with quiet and good visibility. Little grows beneath the dense canopy, so sound carries and travelers are easily spotted. Players enjoy fast travel and peaceful camps, forage for beechnuts and mushrooms, and may stumble onto old shrines or ambushes with few places to hide.
- Oak Woodland: Broad mighty oaks with acorns, sprawling limbs, and boars, birds, and hidden druidic sites. Gnarled branches form arches overhead and the ground is thick with acorns and fallen leaves. Players hunt boar, gather rare mistletoe, and discover druid circles whose keepers may offer aid or fiercely guard their ancient trees.
- Old-Growth Forest: Ancient giant trees, fallen logs, and thick moss in an untouched hush, rich with rare creatures. Light filters faintly through layered canopy, and the air feels timeless and heavy with age. Players encounter unusual beasts, collect rare herbs and fungi, and find that disturbing the forest may provoke guardians or powerful spirits.
- Coppice: Managed woodland of regrowing cut stems, supplying poles and charcoal, with open patches and woodcutters. Clusters of slender shoots sprout from old stumps, and smoke rises from nearby charcoal burners' mounds. Players trade with the workers, collect poles and fuel, and use the bright, open stands for camps, quiet meetings, or snares.
- Hedgerow Country: Patchwork farmland divided by dense hedges and lanes, offering ambush cover, berries, and small wildlife. Sunken lanes wind between thick thorny walls, and fields peek out through gaps. Players follow winding paths to villages and farms, forage wild fruit, and may be surprised by highwaymen, poachers, or folk spirits lurking in the greenery.
- Maple Forest: Sweet-sap trees with brilliant red autumn canopies, sugaring camps, and cool shady trails. In spring sap buckets hang from trunks and steam rises from boiling pans, while autumn paints the woods fiery. Players trade for syrup, hunt game, enjoy beautiful vistas, and may encounter harvest festivals or forest spirits.
- Temperate Rainforest: Misty mossy giants draped in ferns and lichens, dripping with rain, rich in fungi and elusive life. Everything is soaked and green, with slick logs, streams, and rolling fog that muffles sound. Players forage rare mushrooms and herbs, track shy creatures, and risk getting lost or sick as the wet slowly saps supplies.
- Redwood Grove: Colossal towering trunks forming a cathedral-like canopy, with fog, soft ground, and hidden dwellers. Sunbeams slant through mist far above, and the needle-cushioned floor muffles footsteps. Players marvel at monumental trees, meet reclusive hermits or tree-dwelling folk, and may find hollow trunks that serve as shelters, shrines, or secret entrances.
- Bamboo Forest: Dense swaying green stalks creating narrow corridors, rustling sounds, and hiding pandas, assassins, and monks. Light filters in green and creaking stalks clack in the wind, making stealth and sound unpredictable. Players duel in narrow lanes, discover hidden temples, harvest sturdy bamboo, and spot elusive creatures among the swaying thickets.
### Boreal
- Taiga: Vast snowy conifer forest with bogs and lakes, home to wolves, moose, trappers, and long winters. Endless dark spruce and pine roll toward the horizon, with frozen ponds and muskeg between. Players hunt and trap for furs, endure brutal cold and scarce food, and tell stories of wolf packs, lonely cabins, and far-flung trading posts.
- Pine Forest: Resinous needle-carpeted woods with straight trunks, sparse undergrowth, wildfire risk, and fresh scented air. The soft needle floor muffles footsteps and sunlight streaks between tall bare trunks. Players harvest pitch and timber, track game with good sightlines, and must flee or fight the flames when dry lightning or careless camps spark a blaze.
- Spruce-Fir Forest: Dark dense evergreens crowding out sunlight, hushed under snow, harboring lynx, owls, and hunters. Heavy branches droop with snow, and the gloom makes distances and directions hard to judge. Players follow trapline paths, avoid stalking predators, find isolated cabins, and may meet reclusive hunters or spirits that stir in the silent cold.
- Larch Woods: Needled conifers that turn gold and drop in autumn, producing airy light and soft trails. In fall the woods glow amber and carpet the ground in fallen needles, while winter leaves bare, spindly branches. Players enjoy easy travel and bright camps, harvest tough timber, and discover seasonal events, hunting grounds, and quiet places for rituals.
- Birch Forest: Pale-barked slender trees with bright airy canopy, quick regrowth, and folklore full of sprites. White trunks gleam in dim light and leaves shimmer in the breeze, giving a clean, gentle feel. Players gather bark, sap, and mushrooms, encounter forest spirits in fairy tales, and use the open light to spot game and travelers.
### Dry & Mediterranean
- Mediterranean Scrub: Fragrant low thorny shrubs on sunbaked slopes, with herbs, goats, bandits, and wildfire danger. Rosemary, thyme, and gorse scent the hot air, and rocky paths wind between prickly bushes. Players forage herbs, herd goats, and dodge bandits who hide in the brush, while dry summers make a single spark deadly.
- Chaparral: Dense evergreen brush on dry hills, tough to cross, flammable, and home to coyotes and rattlesnakes. Stiff, scratchy shrubs choke the slopes and slow every traveler to a crawl. Players hunt for narrow game trails, endure heat and thirst, and flee fast-moving wildfires that sweep through the tinder-dry growth.
- Cork Oak Dehesa: Open pastureland dotted with cork oaks, grazed by pigs and cattle under hot dry skies. Stripped trunks show rust-red where bark was harvested, and shade pools beneath the spreading limbs. Players trade with herders, hunt acorn-fattened pigs, harvest cork, and meet shepherds or smugglers moving quietly across the sunlit, sparsely guarded land.
- Eucalyptus Forest: Tall aromatic gum trees dropping bark and oily leaves, ideal for fire hazards, koalas, and ghost-like trunks. Pale peeling trunks gleam in the light and branches sometimes fall without warning. Players gather medicinal leaves and honey, spot unusual wildlife, and must outrun explosive fires that race through the oil-rich canopy in dry seasons.
- Dry Woodland: Sparse drought-tolerant trees over dusty ground, with scattered shade, thorny undergrowth, and scarce water. Gnarled trees stand far apart and the soil crunches underfoot. Players ration water, search for hidden springs, and track wary game, while bandits, scavengers, and heat exhaustion make every long crossing a gamble.
- Savanna Woodland: Grassland dotted with broad trees, offering shade, grazing herds, predators, and long sightlines. Wide canopies provide welcome rest from the sun, and animals gather around them at midday. Players hunt, escort caravans, or watch for lions waiting in tall grass, and can use the trees for lookouts and ambushes.
- Miombo Woodland: Wide African tree belt of leafy canopy and dry-season fires, hosting elephants, antelope, and honey gatherers. Light, open woods turn from lush green to golden and smoky as seasons shift. Players follow honey-guide birds to wild hives, avoid elephant herds, trade with villagers, and race the grass fires that sweep across the dry land.
### Forest Features
- Clearing: Open patch within woodland, ideal for camps, ambushes, meetings, and rituals under filtered sunlight. Grass and wildflowers replace the canopy, giving a rare view of the sky and room to move. Players rest, trade, or hold secret gatherings here, though anyone standing in the open is easily seen by hidden watchers at the tree line.
- Glade: Small sunny grassy opening ringed by trees, often peaceful, magical, or home to fae and deer. Soft light, birdsong, and wildflowers give it a calm, enchanted feel. Players can recover here, meet fae courts or gentle wildlife, and find rare herbs, though bargains made in the glade may carry strange costs.
- Forest Edge: Transition zone between woods and open land with scrub, tracks, and wary wildlife. Tall trees give way to brush and fields, and animals pause here before venturing into the open. Players scout from cover, meet travelers on cart tracks, hunt at dawn or dusk, and watch for ambushers using the shadows.
- Forest Floor Undergrowth: Tangled ferns, roots, and leaf litter slowing movement and concealing snakes, mushrooms, and hiding creatures. Every step crunches or snags, and the ground hides holes and slick stones. Players forage fungi and herbs, search for tracks or lost items, and risk stepping on a venomous snake or tripping noisily while stalking prey.
- Fallen Log Maze: Jumble of toppled trunks forming obstacles, hideouts, and winding paths for small creatures and scouts. Moss-covered logs crisscross the ground, making cover and barriers for anyone who crawls through. Players climb, squeeze, and balance through the tangle, finding hidden dens, caches, and shortcuts, while larger foes struggle to follow or surround them.
- Windfall: Area where storms felled trees in tangles, blocking routes while opening sunlight and fresh growth. Roots rise like walls from the torn earth and trunks pile in dangerous heaps. Players cut or climb through to reach the other side, harvest free timber, and find new saplings, nests, and animals seeking shelter among the debris.
- Thicket: Dense knot of brambles and shrubs that snags travelers and offers cover to ambushers. Thorns tug at clothes and armor, and visibility drops to a few paces. Players hack paths through or crawl under the branches, discover berries and hidden animal trails, and risk being surprised by bandits or beasts hiding inside.
- Burned Forest: Charred blackened trunks and ash-covered ground, eerie and open, with sprouting regrowth and displaced creatures. The air smells of smoke and cold cinders, and gray ash puffs with each step. Players find exposed ruins and relics, gather new shoots and fungi, and meet hungry animals, scavengers, or spirits tied to the fire.
- Petrified Forest: Stone-turned tree trunks standing or toppled across a dry landscape, brittle, ancient, and mysterious. Glittering mineral logs lie scattered over barren ground under a wide sky. Players hunt for rare crystals and fossil relics, shelter among the stone trunks, and wonder what ancient event froze the forest, perhaps awakening guardians still stirring within.
- Clearcut: Stumps and scarred earth where loggers stripped the woods, with erosion, slash piles, and angry spirits. The ground is torn, muddy, and littered with branches, and the sun beats down on exposed soil. Players deal with logging crews, scavenge leftover timber, or confront the vengeful nature spirits and displaced creatures haunting the ruined land.
- Sacred Grove: Protected ring of venerable trees with altars, offerings, and guardian spirits tolerating no violence. A deep calm settles over the place, with ribbons, carved stones, and candles left by pilgrims. Players seek blessings, healing, or counsel from keepers, but cutting trees or drawing weapons here invites fierce retribution from the grove's guardians.
- Mushroom Forest: Towering fungi with glowing caps and spore clouds, damp and eerie, home to funguslike beings. Soft, spongy ground and drifting spores make the air thick and the light strange. Players gather rare mushrooms and potion ingredients, befriend or fight spore-folk, and risk hallucinations, poison, or infection from the clouds.
- Foggy Hollow: Low sheltered dip filled with drifting mist, damp air, and secrets, reducing visibility and favoring ambush. Sounds are muffled and shapes loom suddenly out of the gray. Players use the fog to sneak or hide, risk wandering into hidden dangers, and may encounter lost travelers, lurking predators, or ghostly visitors in the murk.
## Grassland & Plains
- Prairie: Wide rolling expanse of grasses and wildflowers under open sky, with herds, wind, and distant storms. Waves of grass ripple to the horizon, and weather can be seen coming for miles. Players ride, hunt bison, and cross dangerous stretches in search of settlements, while sudden thunderstorms and wildfires threaten camps and caravans.
- Tallgrass Prairie: Towering waving blades concealing travelers, bison, and predators, with rich soil and sudden fires. The grass can rise over a rider's head, hiding everything beyond a few steps. Players navigate by sound and wind, stalk or flee unseen creatures, farm the fertile earth, and run from fast-moving prairie fires.
- Shortgrass Steppe: Dry low grass stretching to the horizon, ideal for grazing, riders, and long-distance sight. Little cover exists, so travelers and herds are visible for miles under a hard sun. Players scout, race on horseback, and herd animals, while raiders, dust storms, and scarce water test every long journey.
- Steppe: Cold windy treeless grassland ruled by nomadic horse riders, with vast skies and harsh seasons. Winters freeze the plains and summers scorch them, and endless grass rolls under a huge sky. Players trade with or fight mounted clans, follow migrating herds, shelter in yurts, and survive blizzards and sudden raids.
- Pampas: Flat fertile South American grassland with cattle, gauchos, and strong winds sweeping across the plain. Tall grasses sway under big skies and sparse ombu trees offer rare shade. Players ride with cattle herders, join roundups, face cattle thieves and storms, and share stories and meals around fires at lonely ranches.
- Savanna: Warm grassland with scattered trees, seasonal rains, and great herds hunted by lions and wild hounds. Golden grass and flat-topped trees stretch across heat-shimmered plains, with waterholes drawing crowds. Players follow migrations, hunt or avoid predators, and endure droughts and floods that reshape trails and tribal territories each year.
- Acacia Savanna: Flat-topped thorn trees dotting dry golden grass, providing shade, giraffe browsing, and ambush cover. Long shadows and thorny branches break up the open horizon, and wildlife gathers beneath the canopy. Players rest in the shade, watch for lurking lions, harvest gum and thorns, and find nests, lookouts, and hidden waterholes.
- Veld: Open southern African grassland with kopjes and thorn scrub, home to antelope, farmers, and wary predators. Rocky outcrops rise from rolling grass and the dry air carries dust and distant herds. Players trade with farms, hunt antelope, climb kopjes to scout, and watch for leopards, raiders, or sudden thunderstorms.
- Llanos: Seasonally flooded tropical plains of grass and wetlands, rich in cattle, capybara, caiman, and storms. In the wet season the land becomes a vast shallow lake, and in the dry season it cracks and burns. Players ride cattle drives, wade through flood, and fend off caimans, anacondas, and violent lightning storms.
- Cerrado: Tropical savanna of twisted trees and tough grasses, fire-adapted, with rich wildlife and red soil. Gnarled, thick-barked trees stand in fields of coarse grass that regularly burn and regrow. Players track maned wolves and anteaters, gather unusual fruit and herbs, and must survive scorching fires and hot, dusty travel.
- Outback Scrub Plain: Arid red flatland of spinifex, mulga, and heat, with hardy wildlife and scarce water. Sharp grass clumps and scrubby trees stretch across glaring red earth under a punishing sun. Players hunt for waterholes, avoid venomous snakes, follow sacred paths, and risk dehydration, getting lost, or encountering dangerous drifters.
- Meadow: Gentle grassy field full of flowers, bees, and butterflies, ideal for rest, herbs, and peaceful encounters. Warm sunlight and soft breezes make the place calm and welcoming. Players recover health, gather herbs, meet travelers or fair folk, and enjoy a safe respite, though even meadows can hide snakes, thieves, or surprise visitors.
- Wildflower Field: Colorful carpet of blooms attracting pollinators, perfect for herb gathering, picnics, and fairy folk. Sweet scents fill the air and bees drift lazily between brilliant patches of color. Players collect rare petals and potion herbs, hold festivals or romantic scenes, and may stumble on fairy rings or enchanted visitors.
- Moor: Windswept upland of heather, peat, and mist, with standing stones, sheep, and eerie wandering lights. Boggy ground hides sinkholes, and sudden fog can swallow all landmarks. Players follow faint trails between farms, investigate ruins and stone circles, and fend off ghostly hounds, lost wanderers, and fugitives hiding in the lonely wilds.
- Heathland: Open low shrubland of heather and gorse on poor soil, bleak, purple-blooming, and haunted by fog. Sandy, acidic ground supports little but hardy shrubs, and winds sweep unhindered. Players cross it on old roads, forage berries and honey, hunt grouse, and meet highwaymen, wanderers, or spirits in the drifting mist.
- Downs: Smooth chalky rolling hills with short turf, ancient chalk figures, barrows, and sweeping views. Sheep graze the bare slopes and long ridgelines offer wide sight across the countryside. Players follow ancient trackways, explore burial mounds and giant hillside carvings, and uncover old secrets while exposed to wind and weather.
- Rolling Hills: Gentle green swells of farmland and pasture, dotted with villages, flocks, and winding roads. Hedges, orchards, and stone walls divide the hills, and smoke curls from cottages. Players travel between hamlets, trade at markets, help farmers, and find safe camps, though bandits and wolves still prowl the quieter lanes.
- Plateau Grassland: Elevated flat grassy tableland ringed by escarpments, windswept, with herds and strategic high ground. Cliffs and steep ramps guard the edges while open grass stretches across the top. Players defend or besiege strongholds, herd animals, scout vast distances, and contend with cold winds, sudden storms, and the dangers of descending the slopes.
- Floodplain: Low river-fed flatland rich with silt, lush crops, and seasonal inundations that reshape paths. Fertile fields and reed beds sit beside winding channels, and the land transforms with each flood. Players farm, fish, and travel by boat, but must watch for rising water, stranded villages, mosquitoes, and fever.
- Dune Grassland: Coastal or inland sand ridges stabilized by tough grasses, windy, shifting, and dotted with hollows. Sea oats and marram grass hold the sand while hidden dips shelter small wildlife and travelers. Players hunt for shipwreck debris, shelter from wind, and risk sinking footing, buried ruins, and storms that rearrange the landscape.
- Tussock Land: Clumped tall grass over boggy or cold ground, awkward to walk and hiding small creatures. Spongy mounds with wet gaps between make every step uncertain and exhausting. Players slowly trek across the uneven terrain, search for nests and burrows, and risk twisted ankles, sinking mud, and ambush by creatures that know the clumps.
- Machair: Low-lying coastal grassland of shell sand and wildflowers, wind-battered, with crofts, seabirds, and Atlantic spray. Summer brings a vivid carpet of blooms, while gales whip salt across the flat, treeless land. Players trade with crofters, gather seabird eggs, explore dunes and wrecks, and watch for raiders arriving from the sea.
- Fynbos: South African shrubland of proteas and heath bursting with unique flora, fire-adapted, and endlessly diverse. Colorful flowers and fragrant bushes cover rocky slopes, with sugarbirds darting between blooms. Players gather rare plants for medicine and alchemy, trade with herbalists, and must flee or harness the periodic fires that renew the land.
- Kwongan: Australian sandplain heath of dense shrubs and wildflowers, with extraordinary plant diversity, fire cycles, and little shade. Wildflowers burst across the sandy plains after rain, and the open scrub offers few places to hide. Players search for rare blossoms, honey, and water, endure heat and flies, and survive fire-swept stretches that reshape the terrain.
## Desert & Arid
### Hot Desert
- Sand Sea (Erg): An endless rippling ocean of dunes stretching to every horizon, scorching by day and bitterly cold at night. Sandstorms erase tracks within minutes and scarce oases mark the only safe stops. Players can chase the wreckage of lost caravans, ration water, and navigate by stars.
- Dune Field: Rolling sandy ridges shift with the wind, making every climb slow and tiring, while the reward is a fast sliding descent. Hidden hollows between crests shelter camps, buried relics, and ambushers. Travelers must read wind patterns and avoid exposed crests when sandstorms approach.
- Star Dunes: Towering multi-armed sand pyramids radiate from a central peak, their ridges sharp and steep, and they are visible from miles away. Crossing them is slow and exhausting because the sand collapses underfoot. They make superb landmarks, lookout points, and goals for navigation puzzles or pilgrim routes.
- Rocky Desert (Hamada): A barren plateau of bare stone scoured clean of sand, covered in sharp rubble and baking under glaring heat. There is almost no shade or cover, so travelers are visible for miles. Expect long exposed marches, mineral prospecting, and ruins that wind has stripped bare.
- Gravel Plain (Reg): A flat expanse of tightly packed pebbles that is easy to cross on foot or by vehicle but offers no shade and little shelter. Wind sweeps constantly across it, carrying grit and sound. Caravans favor these routes, making them prime ground for raiders, races, and long chases.
- Salt Flat: A blinding white crust over an ancient dry lakebed, flat to the horizon and glaring enough to cause snow-blindness. Mirages shimmer, and hidden brine pits can swallow the careless. It is ideal for speed-racing, salt harvesting, and stark encounters where nothing hides you.
- Dry Lake (Playa): A cracked clay basin that floods briefly after rain, then bakes flat and hard again. Reflective mirages dance across its surface, and a brief shallow lake can appear overnight. Players can race vehicles across it, find stranded wrecks, or time a visit with rare flash blooms.
- Mesa and Butte: Flat-topped rock towers and cliffs rise abruptly from the desert floor, their layered walls glowing red at sunset. The summits offer high ground, wide views, and defensible positions. Expect hidden ruins, cliff dwellings, and lookout posts reached by narrow climbs or ancient stairways.
- Canyonlands: A maze of red rock gorges, arches, and layered cliffs carved by ancient water and wind. Hidden springs, cliff ruins, and shaded side canyons reward explorers, while narrow bends create perfect ambush spots. Getting lost is easy, and flash floods threaten anyone camping on the canyon floor.
- Slot Canyon: A tight winding corridor carved by flash floods, with smooth sculpted walls and shafts of filtered light that glow amber and orange. Passages can narrow until you must squeeze sideways. Sudden water surges from distant storms are deadly, so every trek becomes a race against the sky.
- Wadi: A dry streambed that usually lies silent and parched but floods suddenly when rain falls far upstream. It serves as a natural travel route, offering shade under its banks and occasional waterholes. Players must watch the horizon for storms and decide when to camp or flee.
- Oasis: A lush spring-fed island of palms, reeds, and clear pools set in barren sands. Caravans, nomads, bandits, and wildlife all converge here, making it a natural trading post and flashpoint. Players can resupply, trade rumors, broker truces, or defend the water source against thirsty raiders.
- Desert Scrub: Sparse hardy shrubs and thorny plants dot sandy gravel soil, offering thin cover and scattered forage. Lizards, snakes, and wiry coyotes hunt among the bushes, and nights turn cold. Foragers can find edible seeds and medicinal herbs, but they must watch for venomous ambushes.
- Cactus Forest: Dense stands of towering spined giants and prickly pears cover the hills, with birds nesting in their arms and spines waiting to snag cloth and skin. Cacti store precious water that can save a thirsty traveler. Players harvest fruit and fluid while avoiding thorns and nesting predators.
- Desert Pavement: A smooth mosaic of tightly packed stones polished by wind over centuries, forming a hard flat surface that is easy to travel across. Little grows here and the ground glints under the sun. It leaves clear footprints in places, so trackers and scouts can follow or hide trails.
- Yardang Field: Wind-sculpted ridges of soft rock stand aligned like the hulls of overturned ships, separated by narrow sandy corridors. The howling wind produces eerie sounds as it funnels through. These formations create natural mazes and cover in an otherwise barren expanse, ideal for hideouts and ambushes.
- Mirage Flats: Shimmering heat-warped plains where illusions of water, cities, and distant travelers float above the horizon. Thirsty wanderers are lured off course into confusion and exhaustion. Players might chase false oases, follow genuine hidden ruins glimpsed through the haze, or be misled by treacherous guides.
### Cold & Coastal Desert
- Cold Desert Basin: A high dry bowl ringed by distant mountains, with freezing nights, sparse sagebrush, and bitter winds that rarely let up. Rain is scarce and temperatures swing wildly between day and night. Travelers must carry warm gear and water, and may find lonely ranches, ghost towns, or hidden mineral wealth.
- Gobi Steppe-Desert: A windy stony expanse of sparse grass and shifting sand where weather can turn from warm to freezing in hours. Nomads herd camels and horses between seasonal camps. Players can join caravans, trade with herders, hunt for fossils, and survive sudden cold snaps and dust storms.
- Antarctic Dry Valleys: Frozen windswept rock valleys free of snow, where ancient ice lies buried and the silence is almost total. Katabatic winds scour the ground, and mummified seals and penguins lie preserved for centuries. Explorers can seek hidden lakes, frozen relics, and strange microbial life in this alien landscape.
- Coastal Fog Desert: An arid shore watered only by drifting sea fog that rolls in each morning, nourishing specialized plants, quiet beaches, and towering dunes. Rain almost never falls, yet life clings on in the mist. Players can catch fog water, forage hardy lichens, and glimpse rare creatures through the gloom.
- Skeleton Coast: A fog-wrapped shore littered with shipwrecks, whale bones, and shifting dunes, pounded by relentless surf and treacherous currents. Navigation is dangerous and rescue nearly impossible, so stranded sailors rarely survive. Players can salvage wrecks, uncover cargo and grim secrets, and race to find water before it is too late.
## Mountain & Alpine
### Peaks & Ridges
- Summit: The highest point of a mountain, where air is thin, winds are fierce, and the view stretches across whole ranges. Reaching it demands endurance, planning, and often a final dangerous push. It is a natural place for a triumphant reward, a shrine, a final boss, or a hard-earned moment of reflection.
- Ridge: A narrow crest between two slopes, exposed to punishing wind and sudden falls on either side. It offers sweeping sightlines for scouts and archers, but every step requires balance and nerve. Players can use ridgelines as fast routes or defensive lanes, while storms and ambushes make them deadly.
- Arete: A sharp knife-edge rock spine separating two valleys, requiring careful climbing with rope and steady nerves. Dizzying drops plunge on both sides, and loose rock or ice can give way without warning. Crossing one makes a tense set-piece for climbing mechanics, pursuit scenes, or a duel with no room to retreat.
- Mountain Pass: A high saddle route between valleys, serving as a strategic choke point for trade, armies, and migrations. Weather changes fast, snow can close it for months, and bandits or tolls often wait at the narrowest points. Players can fortify it, escort caravans through, or fight over control of the route.
- Saddle: A low dip between two peaks, offering an easier crossing than the surrounding heights but funneling wind into a constant gale. The sheltered sides provide good campsites and meeting places. Travelers rest here before the final climb, and rivals often cross paths or set ambushes in the same spot.
- Cliff Face: A sheer vertical rock wall demanding climbing skill, with narrow ledges, nesting birds, and loose holds that crumble under weight. Falling hazards and rockslides threaten anyone below. Players can scale it to reach hidden caves and eyries, rappel down for surprise attacks, or search for ancient carvings and handholds.
- Overhang: A protruding rock ceiling that forces awkward, strength-draining climbs and hangs over empty air. It can shelter caves, camps, and hidden stashes, but it may also collapse without warning. Players can use it for cover from rain and rockfall, or face tense climbs where slipping means a long drop.
- Spire: A slender jagged pinnacle of rock that thrusts skyward, difficult to climb and prone to lightning strikes. Wind howls around its needle-like tip, and ledges are tiny. It makes an ideal lair for hermits, wizards, or monsters, and a striking landmark for a quest or a dramatic confrontation.
- Massif: A huge compact mountain mass with multiple peaks, deep valleys, and glaciers, forming a rugged natural fortress. Few routes lead through it, so whole cultures can hide inside its folds. Players explore hidden valleys, contested passes, and mining settlements, while the sheer scale makes any journey a long expedition.
- Mountain Plateau: A high flat tableland above the surrounding slopes, windswept, cold, and open to wide skies. Few trees grow here, but grazing herds and hidden settlements thrive. Players might find isolated villages, ancient ruins, or nomadic herders, and can face fierce storms with nowhere to shelter.
### Slopes
- Scree Slope: A steep field of loose small stones that slide underfoot with every step, making noise that carries across the mountain. Climbing is tiring and descending is dangerous, with sudden slips and tumbling rocks. Players can use the slide for fast descents or ambush noisy climbers, but injuries are common.
- Talus: An apron of fallen rock fragments piled at the base of a cliff, offering rough uneven footing and countless small crevices. Small animals such as pikas and marmots hide among the stones. Players can scavenge for fallen gear and minerals, but should beware of unstable stones and rockfall from above.
- Boulder Field: An expanse of large angular rocks requiring constant scrambling, with deep gaps between them and ankle-twisting hazards at every step. The boulders provide ample cover for skirmishes and ambushes. Movement slows to a crawl, vehicles cannot cross, and hidden caves or nests can hide treasure or predators.
- Avalanche Chute: A steep gully scoured clean by sliding snow, dangerous to cross and littered with debris fans of broken trees and rock at its base. A sudden slide can bury travelers in seconds. Players must time crossings, avoid noise, and may trigger avalanches deliberately to crush enemies or clear a path.
- Rock Glacier: A slow-creeping mass of rubble and ice that groans and shifts as it moves downhill, with unstable footing and hidden voids beneath the stones. Meltwater runs below the surface and cold air seeps from cracks. Players can find preserved relics frozen inside, but crossing risks sudden collapse.
- Moraine: A long ridge of rubble and boulders deposited by a glacier, offering rough cover and natural defensive lines. The ground is loose and unstable, and it often overlooks a cold meltwater lake. Players can use moraines as barricades, and may find ancient ice-carried artifacts or boulders strangely out of place.
- Alpine Meadow: A flower-filled high pasture above the trees, bright with wildflowers, clear streams, and grazing animals such as marmots and goats. The air is brisk and fresh, and the views are spectacular. It offers a peaceful rest stop, a place to gather herbs and meet herders, or a picturesque trap.
- Krummholz Zone: Stunted wind-twisted trees cling to the mountainside near the treeline, forming dense low thickets that are difficult to push through. Branches grow sideways away from the prevailing wind. The tangled growth offers cover for ambushers and small animals, but it slows travelers and tears gear as they struggle through.
- Treeline: The border where forest gives way to bare rock and tundra, marking sharp changes in temperature, cover, and wildlife. Below it, shelter and firewood are plentiful, while above it the wind bites and nothing hides you. Players often make final preparations here, or stage camps before pushing higher.
- Subalpine Forest: Sparse hardy conifers cling to high slopes, separated by snow patches and rocky clearings and buffeted by cold winds. Elk, mountain goats, and wolves roam between the trees. Players can hunt, gather timber, and find shelter, but sudden storms and thin air make every journey harder than it looks.
- Montane Forest: Cool misty mid-elevation woods of tall trees rich in ferns, mosses, and tumbling streams. Fog drifts between the trunks, muffling sound and hiding movement. Elusive mountain creatures such as bears, lynxes, and rare birds roam here, and players can find hidden shrines, charcoal burners, and sheltered trails.
- Snowfield: A broad white expanse of packed snow that glares brightly under the sun and offers no cover. Hidden crevasses lurk beneath thin snow bridges, and snow blindness threatens anyone without goggles. Players must rope up, probe ahead, and watch the weather, and may find frozen travelers or caches buried below.
- Cornice: An overhanging lip of wind-packed snow along a ridge that looks solid but collapses under weight, hiding the drop beneath. Travelers who walk too close fall with the snow. Players can trigger cornices to bury pursuers, or must navigate carefully along the safe side of the ridge and avoid the edge.
- Ice Wall: A vertical frozen face that demands ice axes, crampons, and rope, glassy and bitterly cold to the touch. Falling chunks of ice and sudden cracks make every move risky. Players climb it to reach hidden ledges and summits, and sometimes discover relics or creatures trapped within the translucent ice.
### Valleys
- U-Shaped Valley: A broad glacier-carved trough with steep walls and a flat floor, often holding lakes, braided rivers, and tall waterfalls tumbling from the cliffs. The flat floor suits farms and settlements, while the walls offer lookouts. Players can find thriving villages, trade routes, and dramatic sites carved by ancient ice.
- Cirque: A bowl-shaped glacial hollow ringed by steep cliffs on three sides, often holding a still lake, lingering snow, and secluded camps. It is a natural dead end with spectacular views. Players might find hidden sanctuaries, ambush sites, or a dramatic starting point for a climb up the headwall.
- Tarn: A small cold mountain lake nestled in a high hollow, so clear and still that it mirrors the peaks above. Often considered sacred, it may hide sunken secrets, offerings, or lurking creatures. Players can fish for rare trout, perform rituals, or dive for relics, but the freezing water is dangerous.
- Hanging Valley: A side valley that ends abruptly in a cliff high above the main valley floor, producing dramatic waterfalls that plunge into the depths. Its isolated position makes it a perfect hidden refuge. Players can discover secret settlements, rare plants, or lost shrines, reached only by hard climbs or hidden paths.
- Gorge: A deep narrow chasm cut by a river, with steep walls, roaring water, and spray that fills the air. Bridges and ferry crossings are rare and strategically important. Players can fight over crossing points, ride rapids, or explore cliffside caves, while ambushers loom on the rim above.
- Ravine: A steep narrow cleft carved by running water, offering cover, hidden trails, and shady places to rest. Its walls channel sound and block sight lines, perfect for ambushes and escapes. After heavy rain, flash floods can sweep through without warning, trapping unwary travelers in a rushing torrent.
### Mountain Types
- Fold Mountains: Great crumpled ranges with long parallel ridges and valleys, formed when colliding plates buckle the earth into waves. They are rich in minerals and metals, attracting miners and warring factions. Players follow valleys between ridges, find hidden ore veins, and cross passes that connect isolated kingdoms.
- Block Mountains: Abrupt ranges lifted along faults, with steep scarps on one side and gentler slopes on the other. The dramatic cliff face acts as a natural wall, while the long slope offers approach routes. Players can defend or assault fortifications built along the scarp, and find hot springs along the fault lines.
- Karst Mountains: Eroded limestone peaks riddled with caves, sinkholes, and vanishing streams that disappear underground. The terrain is rugged and sharp, with hidden chambers beneath every slope. Perfect for secret dungeons, underground rivers, and lost civilizations, though a misstep can drop explorers into deep dark shafts.
- Tower Karst: Dramatic isolated limestone pillars rise steeply from flat plains or water, often jungle-clad with vines hanging from the cliffs. Caves, ledges, and hidden hideouts honeycomb the sides. Players can climb them for hidden refuges and pagodas, glide between them, or hunt rare creatures isolated on the summits.
- Sky Island Mountain: An isolated peak rising from very different surrounding terrain, hosting its own distinct climate, unique plants and creatures, and cut-off cultures. The summit feels like a separate world, with its own weather and rules. Players can climb through changing biomes and discover species found nowhere else.
- Inselberg: A solitary rocky hill rising abruptly from a flat plain, visible from great distances and impossible to miss. It serves as a landmark, refuge, or sacred site, with caves and ledges around its base. Players can use it as a navigation point, a lookout, or a hideout in open country.
- Tepui: A flat-topped, sheer-walled table mountain draped in waterfalls and swirling mist, with strange isolated life on its summit. The cliffs are hard to climb and the top feels like a lost world. Players can search for unique species, forgotten ruins, and treasure sealed away from the outside world.
- Tors: Rough granite outcrops crown moorland hills, weathered into stacks of rounded blocks and narrow cracks. Mist and wind sweep over them, and local legends cling to every stone. Players can use tors as lookouts and shelters, investigate folklore sites and standing stones, or face ambushes in the foggy moors.
- Foothills: Low rolling ridges at the base of a mountain range, dotted with woods, streams, and pastures. They serve as a gateway to higher country, where travelers gather supplies and hire guides. Players can find villages, trade posts, and bandit camps, and enjoy gentle terrain before the real climb begins.
- Altiplano: A high cold windy plateau between mountain ranges, with thin air, salt lakes, llamas, and scattered villages. The sky is vast and the nights are freezing. Travelers struggle with altitude sickness but can trade with herders, mine salt and minerals, or cross spectacular flats with distant volcanic peaks.
- Death Zone: An extreme altitude where there is too little oxygen for the body to survive long, and every hour weakens the climber. Storms, frostbite, and exhaustion are constant threats, and bodies of earlier climbers lie preserved. Players must manage supplies, speed, and fatal decisions, knowing one mistake ends the expedition.
## Wetland & Swamp
- Swamp: A waterlogged forest of knotted roots, stagnant pools, and hanging vines, thick with mosquitoes, snakes, and slow-moving predators. Visibility is poor and footing treacherous. Players wade through murky water to find hidden huts, herbs, and relics, while disease and ambushes make every step a gamble.
- Cypress Swamp: Tall buttressed cypress trees draped in moss stand in dark still water, their knobby knee roots poking above the surface. Eerie fog hangs between the trunks and alligators lurk below. Players can pole boats through winding channels, find hidden shrines and moonshiners' stills, and meet ghostly legends.
- Mangrove Swamp: A tangled thicket of stilt-rooted trees in brackish tidal water, flooding and draining with the tides. Crabs, snakes, and fish hide among the roots, and smugglers' boats slip through hidden channels. Players can navigate by tide, ambush from cover, and discover nurseries teeming with life.
- Bayou: Slow sluggish backwater channels wind through low forest draped in moss, home to alligators, fishermen, and rickety stilt shacks. Mist rises at dawn and cicadas drone at dusk. Players can pole through narrow waterways, trade with locals, uncover smuggler caches, and encounter folklore creatures and haunted plantations.
- Bog: A spongy acidic wetland of sphagnum moss that swallows footsteps and preserves whatever sinks into it, including old bodies, relics, and weapons. The ground quakes underfoot and hides sudden pits. Players can dig for ancient treasure and peat, but they risk sinking and unearthing nasty surprises.
- Peat Bog: Dark springy ground made of decayed moss, cut and harvested for fuel by local communities. Hidden sinking pockets and water-filled holes riddle the surface, and the smell of damp smoke lingers. Players can work the peat cuttings, uncover preserved remains, and keep to marked paths to survive.
- Pitcher Plant Bog: An open acidic wetland dotted with giant carnivorous plants whose slippery cups trap insects and small creatures. The air hums with flies and the ground is soggy and nutrient poor. Players can harvest rare plant nectar and fluids for alchemy, but must avoid the larger specimens that devour careless victims.
- Fen: An alkaline marshland of sedges and reeds fed by groundwater, rich in rare flowers, orchids, birds, and insects. Hidden paths wind through the tall growth, known only to locals. Players can forage rare herbs, hunt wildfowl, and guide travelers across safe routes, while will-o'-the-wisp legends haunt the misty nights.
- Marsh: A flat grassy wetland of shallow water and reeds, full of waterfowl, frogs, and insects. Tall vegetation provides easy ambush cover while slowing movement through mud and water. Players can hunt, fish, and gather reeds, or conduct stealth raids, though flooded channels and biting insects make travel miserable.
- Salt Marsh: A tidal grassland flooded by seawater twice daily, laced with winding creeks, mud flats, and wading birds. Tides change the landscape quickly, cutting off routes and revealing others. Players must time their travel, harvest salt and samphire, and avoid being stranded by rising water at dusk.
- Freshwater Marsh: Reed-choked shallows of cattails and lilies teeming with fish, ducks, muskrats, and biting insects. The water is warm and still, and the vegetation grows thick and tall. Players can hunt and trap for food, gather reeds and rushes for crafting, and use boats to slip unseen through channels.
- Reed Bed: Dense walls of tall rustling stalks line the water's edge, whispering in the wind and hiding everything within. They are ideal for concealing boats, nests, and ambushers. Players can harvest reeds for thatch and boats, stage ambushes from within, or set fire to the beds to flush out enemies.
- Floodplain Forest: Lowland woods that seasonally drown under river overflow, leaving rich silt, driftwood, and muddy trails. Trees stand in water for months, then dry out into fertile ground. Players can farm the rich soil, salvage flood debris, and plan travel around the water levels, as the landscape changes with each season.
- Sawgrass Everglades: An endless river of grass and shallow water broken by tree islands, alligators, and razor-edged blades that slice skin. The sky is huge and the horizon flat. Players can airboat across the glades, hide on tree islands, hunt rare wildlife, and face storms and fire that sweep the open grass.
- Vernal Pool: A temporary seasonal pond that appears after rain, brimming with amphibian eggs, fairy shrimp, and rare specialized life. It dries out completely in summer, leaving cracked mud. Players can time visits to witness choruses of frogs, gather rare ingredients, and protect a fragile habitat from careless destruction.
- Wet Meadow: Soggy grassland with standing puddles and wildflowers, good for grazing but difficult for heavy loads. Frogs, snipes, and dragonflies thrive here, and the ground squelches underfoot. Players can graze livestock, gather medicinal plants, and slow pursuers by luring wagons or cavalry into the mud.
- Quagmire: Treacherous soft mud and half-liquid soil that traps feet, wagons, and animals, sucking them down slowly. It looks like ordinary ground until someone steps in. Careful scouts are rewarded with safe routes and shortcuts, while careless travelers lose gear or lives, and enemies can be lured into it deliberately.
- Quicksand Pit: A deceptive patch of liquefied sand that pulls victims under when they struggle, often hidden beneath a thin crust. It is a classic hidden trap hazard in deserts, riverbeds, and swamps. Players can escape by staying calm and spreading their weight, and may rescue companions with ropes or poles.
- Mire: Deep clinging mud and foul water where movement crawls, fog lingers, and lost travelers vanish. The air smells of rot, and strange lights flicker across the surface at night. Players face slow travel, disease, and creatures that lurk below, but may find lost treasure and hidden refuges.
- Delta Wetland: A sprawling fan of braided channels, islets, and silt flats where a great river meets the sea. Water levels and channels shift each season, creating a constantly changing maze. Players can navigate by boat, establish trading ports on islands, and hunt rich bird and fish populations amid frequent floods.
- Pocosin: An elevated shrubby peat wetland of dense evergreen thickets, black water, and tangled vines that make passage difficult. Bears, snakes, and other wildlife thrive in the near-impenetrable growth. Few easy trails exist, so players need local guides, risk getting lost, and may stumble on hidden stills and wildfire-charred areas.
- Muskeg: A northern bog of floating moss mats and stunted spruce, hard to cross and swarming with biting flies in summer. The ground bounces and can give way, while freezing winters turn it into passable ice. Players can plan seasonal crossings, trap furs, and search for hidden lakes beneath the moss.
- Blackwater Swamp: Tea-dark tannin-stained water lies beneath a dense canopy, hiding sunken logs, silent hunters, and murky depths. Light barely penetrates and reflections look like mirrors. Players can navigate by boat or wade carefully, harvest rare fish and plants, and face ambushes from unseen creatures lurking below the surface.
## Tundra & Polar
- Arctic Tundra: Treeless frozen plain of lichen, low shrubs, and meltwater pools, roamed by caribou, wolves, and hardy nomads. Summer brings swarming insects and boggy ground while winter brings months of darkness and howling wind. Players hunt migrating herds, trade with nomad camps, and must ration warmth and food to survive the long season.
- Alpine Tundra: Windswept high-altitude zone above the tree line with cushion plants, bare rock, marmots, and thin air. Weather turns violent within minutes, and travelers tire quickly in the cold, oxygen-poor air. Players cross passes, forage rare herbs, and discover shrines or abandoned waystations tucked among the boulders.
- Antarctic Tundra: Barren southern coast of moss and rock beaten by gales, home to penguins and seals. Colonies crowd the shingle beaches, while the interior offers almost no shelter, fuel, or plants. Expeditions land here to resupply, hunt for stranded wrecks, and uncover research huts left behind by earlier explorers.
- Polar Ice Cap: Vast permanent dome of ice at the world's pole, blindingly white with lethal cold. Snow glare, whiteouts, and sub-zero winds make navigation brutal, and magnetic compasses may spin unreliably. Players trek toward distant poles, hunt for crashed craft or frozen outposts, and race against exposure and dwindling supplies.
- Ice Sheet: Continent-sized slab of ancient ice, miles thick, flat and featureless, with buried secrets and crevasses. Katabatic winds scour the surface and hidden fissures lurk under thin snow. Explorers drill for ancient cores, find objects entombed for millennia, and must navigate by stars or instruments across a landscape without landmarks.
- Glacier: Slow river of blue ice grinding down valleys, cracked by crevasses and carrying boulders and frozen relics. It groans and shifts constantly, and meltwater streams cut channels across its surface. Players cross it with ropes and spikes, mine its ice for water, and may find lost travelers or ancient artifacts released by the melt.
- Ice Shelf: Floating platform of glacier ice jutting over the sea, prone to sudden calving and cracking. Its flat surface looks safe, yet the edge can shear off without warning, dropping camps into freezing water. Players build bases or hunt seals here, always weighing the risk of collapse against the rich fishing below.
- Ice Floe Field: Scattered drifting slabs of sea ice requiring risky hops, with freezing water and hunting predators beneath. The floes tilt, spin, and crack under weight, and the gaps between them can close or widen in moments. Players leap across for hunting or escape while avoiding polar bears and orcas that stalk from the water.
- Pack Ice: Crushed jumble of grinding ice sheets that traps ships, groans constantly, and shifts without warning. Pressure ridges rise and collapse as floes collide, making both sailing and walking treacherous. Crews may be stranded for months, forced to scavenge, melt ice for water, and survive the creaking, crushing vessel around them.
- Iceberg: Towering floating mass of glacial ice, mostly hidden below water, hazardous to ships and sometimes hollow. Its sheer glittering walls drift slowly with the currents and may roll over without warning. Players can land on one to harvest pure ice, explore its internal caverns, or discover a frozen wreck embedded in the blue interior.
- Frozen Sea: Ocean surface locked solid into a walkable plain of ice with pressure ridges and thin patches. In deep winter it becomes a highway connecting islands and coasts, but dark leads and hidden cracks can swallow sleds whole. Players cross it with caravans, fish through holes, and avoid raiders who strike across the open white.
- Permafrost Plain: Ground frozen year-round beneath a thin thawing layer, producing mud, sinking bogs, and preserved remains. Summer turns the surface to sucking mire while buildings tilt and roads buckle. Players dig up mammoth bones and frozen relics, struggle to build lasting settlements, and risk releasing buried things that were better left locked in ice.
- Pingo Field: Scattered ice-cored earth domes rising from tundra flats, useful as lookouts and natural landmarks. Each mound is a hill of soil wrapped around a growing lens of ice, and some collapse into crater ponds as they melt. Players use them as scouting points and campsites, or break into their cores to find hidden caches.
- Patterned Ground: Strange polygon and stripe formations in frozen soil, creating eerie geometric landscapes and uneven footing. Freeze and thaw cycles sort stones into rings and lines that look deliberately made. Travelers find it slow to cross, while superstitious locals treat it as sacred, and players may mistake it for ruins or a message from earlier inhabitants.
- Thermokarst: Thawing frozen ground collapsing into sinkholes, ponds, and slumping hollows that release trapped gas and ancient remains. The landscape is lumpy and unstable, with sudden drops and bubbling methane pockets that can ignite. Players scavenge freshly exposed bones and artifacts but risk falling into hidden voids or sinking into unstable, steaming mud.
- Snow Plain: Wide blanket of drifted snow with little shelter, prone to whiteouts, tracking, and exposure. Wind sculpts the surface into drifts that hide obstacles and erase footprints within hours. Players can follow tracks to prey or enemies, dig snow shelters against storms, and must keep moving or find cover before the cold wins.
- Frozen Lake: Smooth sheet of ice over deep water, fast to cross but dangerous where thin or cracking. It booms and echoes under pressure, and springs or currents create weak patches that look identical to solid ice. Players ice fish, skate across for shortcuts, stage duels or festivals, and sometimes fall through to meet whatever lurks below.
- Ice Cave: Glittering blue chambers carved inside glaciers by meltwater, with slippery floors, falling icicles, and hibernating beasts. Light filters through the walls in eerie shades, and echoes carry far. Players seek shelter from storms, harvest crystal-clear ice, and risk waking bears or worse while exploring frozen tunnels that may collapse behind them.
- Crevasse Field: Maze of deep hidden cracks beneath snow bridges, demanding ropes, probing, and nerves. Thin crusts conceal drops of hundreds of feet, and the ice creaks with every step. Players must rope together, probe ahead, and plot careful routes, while rescues, lost cargo, and frozen bodies below offer both danger and discovery.
- Nunatak: Lone rocky peak poking through an ice sheet, serving as camp, landmark, and refuge for hardy life. Lichens, mosses, and nesting birds cling to the wind-scoured stone, and the rock offers bare shelter from the ice winds. Expeditions use it as a waypoint or base, and strange shrines or caches often wait on the summit.
- Sastrugi Field: Wind-carved ridges of hard snow forming a jagged washboard that slows sleds and twists ankles. The waves of frozen crust run in the direction of prevailing wind and can reach knee height. Players use their orientation to navigate in whiteouts, but crossing them wears out dogs, vehicles, and travelers alike.
- Icefield: Broad upland expanse of connected glaciers and snow, ringed by peaks and dotted with hidden meltwater channels. Its high, wind-blasted surface feeds many glaciers and rivers below. Players cross it to reach remote valleys, search for hidden meltwater lakes, and risk sudden storms, crevasses, and avalanches rolling down from the surrounding summits.
## Freshwater
### Rivers & Streams
- Headwater Stream: Small clear brook trickling from springs or snowmelt high in the hills, cold and easy to wade. Moss, ferns, and smooth stones line its banks, and its water is usually safe to drink. Players follow it upstream to find hidden springs, shrines, or hermits, or downstream as a guide toward settlements and larger rivers.
- Brook: Shallow babbling creek over pebbles, good for fishing, drinking water, and quietly following to settlements. Its gentle sound masks approaching footsteps, and its banks hold berries, frogs, and herbs. Players camp beside it, pan for small gold flecks, or trace its course through woods to find mills, farms, and quiet hideouts.
- Mountain Torrent: Fast, icy, boulder-strewn water crashing down slopes, with deadly currents and few safe crossings. The roar drowns out speech, and spray makes rocks dangerously slick. Players hunt for narrow log bridges or rope crossings, harness the power for mills and forges, and risk being swept away by sudden snowmelt floods.
- Meandering River: Broad looping river with slow bends, sandbars, and rich banks, ideal for boats and settlements. Silt-fed floodplains grow thick crops and reeds, while oxbows and fish-filled shallows line the route. Players trade by barge, build riverside towns, and fight over bridges and ferries, though seasonal floods can reshape the whole valley.
- Braided River: Wide gravel bed split into many shifting channels, tricky to ford and constantly changing course. After storms the paths rearrange, and quicksand-like silt hides in some channels. Players must scout carefully to cross with wagons or herds, and the barren bars make poor shelter but offer open sightlines for travelers and ambushers.
- River Delta: Fan of silty channels and islands at the river's mouth, fertile farmland with ports and marshes. Countless waterways split and rejoin, creating a maze of reeds, fishing villages, and trade barges. Players navigate by boat, smuggle goods through hidden channels, or contend with floods, mosquitoes, and rival harbor towns controlling the outlets.
- Rapids: Churning whitewater over rocks that wrecks careless boats, demands portaging, and marks natural chokepoints. The noise is constant, and foam hides submerged stones that can tear a hull. Players shoot the rapids for speed, lug cargo around them, or ambush travelers forced to land at the portage trails.
- Waterfall: Thundering plunge over a cliff edge with mist, hidden caves behind the curtain, and a plunge pool. Rainbows hang in the spray, and the roar carries for miles. Players can climb beside it, discover secret chambers behind the falling water, dive into the pool for treasure, or find it guarded as a sacred site.
- Cataract: Great stepped series of falls and rapids blocking river travel, often guarded by forts or toll keepers. Boats must be unloaded and hauled overland, so towns and portage roads spring up on either side. Players bargain or fight for passage, run smuggling operations around the toll, or attempt a reckless descent that few survive.
- Whitewater Gorge: Narrow rock canyon funneling violent river flow between sheer walls, thrilling for rafting and ambushes. The water thunders through twisting bends with little room to escape, and ledges high above overlook the channel. Players raft through for speed or glory, hide caches in cliff caves, and stage attacks on anything forced through.
- Oxbow Lake: Crescent-shaped lake cut off from a river's old bend, calm, stagnant, and rich with fish. Lily pads, reeds, and herons crowd the quiet water, while mud and mosquitoes ring the banks. Players fish and gather reeds, find old riverbed relics, and may stumble on hermits, smuggler camps, or something lurking in the still, dark water.
- Backwater: Still side channel off a main river, sheltered for boats, thick with weeds, insects, and hidden camps. Slow water collects driftwood and mud, and the tangled banks hide anyone who wishes to remain unseen. Players moor boats here away from the current, ambush river traffic, or find abandoned shacks and stashed goods.
- Ford: Shallow river crossing over gravel or stone, a strategic chokepoint for armies, caravans, and bandit tolls. Water depth shifts with the seasons, and flood or drought may close it altogether. Players defend or seize the crossing, haggle with toll collectors, and watch for ambushes while wagons slowly and noisily splash across.
- River Island: Mid-stream patch of land shielded by moving water, suited to hideouts, small keeps, and fishing camps. Willows and driftwood pile on its shores, and the surrounding current gives natural defense. Players claim it for a secret base, a toll tower, or a hermitage, but must watch for rising floods that can swallow it overnight.
### Lakes & Ponds
- Alpine Lake: Crystal cold mountain lake ringed by peaks, usually still and deep, with trout and snowmelt feeders. The water mirrors the sky and surrounding rock, and the shoreline is sparse with stunted pines and meadow flowers. Players camp here, fish, or search for shrines and sunken treasures, while sudden storms and freezing water punish the unprepared.
- Rift Lake: Long, steep-sided deep lake in a tectonic trough, with dramatic cliffs and strange endemic fish. Its depths hold species found nowhere else, and volcanic vents or earthquakes can stir the waters. Players sail its length for trade, study unusual wildlife, and may find ancient settlements built along its sheltered, fertile shores.
- Crater Lake: Round lake filling an extinct volcano's bowl, intensely blue, with steep walls and sacred reputation. Few streams enter, so the water is clear, deep, and eerily still. Players climb down the rim to reach pilgrim shrines, dive for offerings, or investigate rumors that something dormant still stirs beneath the surface.
- Glacial Lake: Milky meltwater pool dammed by ice or moraine, freezing, silty, and prone to sudden outburst floods. Its turquoise color hides bitter cold, and icebergs calve from the ice edge into the water. Players camp on its shores and harvest ice, but a dam failure can send a destructive wall of water roaring down the valley.
- Salt Lake: Landlocked saline water with bright crusted shores and brine shrimp, useless to drink but valuable for salt. White flats glare in the sun, and the air shimmers with heat and sharp mineral smell. Players harvest salt for trade, find odd life adapted to the brine, and struggle to find fresh water nearby.
- Soda Lake: Caustic alkaline lake with flamingos and eerie coloring, whose chemistry harms travelers yet supports odd life. Pink and red hues bloom across its surface, and the shores are crusted with strange crystals. Players risk burns from the water, harvest minerals for alchemy, and watch for creatures that thrive where nothing else can.
- Reservoir: Man-made lake held by a dam, supplying water and power, with drowned buildings and a vulnerable wall. Old roofs and towers may poke above low water, and the dam itself is a tempting strategic target. Players explore sunken villages, defend or sabotage the dam, and decide whether to open the gates and flood the valley below.
- Pond: Small still pool of lilies, frogs, and dragonflies, suitable for fishing, watering livestock, and quiet rest. Cattails and mud ring its edges, and the calm water reflects the sky. Players gather herbs, fish, or rest and heal here, though it can hide leeches, a lurking pike, or a fey presence in local tales.
- Mere: Broad shallow lake bordered by reeds and low fen, often misty and haunted by local legend. Fog rolls across the water at dawn, and boggy shores make landing uncertain. Players pole flat boats among reed islands, hunt waterfowl, and uncover stories of drowned villages, lantern lights, and something old sleeping beneath the muddy bed.
- Subglacial Lake: Hidden body of liquid water trapped beneath ice, isolated for ages and holding strange life. Geothermal heat and pressure keep it from freezing, and no light has touched it for millennia. Players drill or tunnel down to reach it, risking collapse and contamination, to find unknown organisms, ancient secrets, or a sealed ecosystem.
- Lake Island: Small wooded or rocky isle in open water, natural site for hermits, shrines, and fortified retreats. The surrounding water offers defense and solitude, and reaching it requires a boat or hidden causeway. Players seek out its monks, outlaws, or ruined keep, and may find themselves stranded when storms or hostile rowers cut off escape.
### Springs
- Spring: Natural fount of clean water bubbling from rock, a vital oasis and often a sacred or contested site. Green growth crowds around the cool pool, drawing animals and travelers alike. Players refill supplies, rest, and heal here, while factions fight over control, pilgrims leave offerings, and some springs carry strange curative or cursed properties.
- Artesian Well: Pressurized groundwater rising unaided to the surface, forming a reliable oasis in otherwise dry lands. Water gushes or bubbles up without pumping, sustaining a ring of green in barren country. Players find caravans and settlements clustered around it, and conflict arises as raiders, engineers, and rival tribes vie to control the precious flow.
- Seep: Slow trickle of water oozing from a hillside, creating a damp green patch and tiny life zone. Moss, ferns, and insects cluster around the wet rock, and the water may be slightly mineral or muddy. Players can survive on it in a pinch, track animals that visit, and sometimes find hidden caves or springs behind the mossy curtain.
## Ocean & Coastal
### Shoreline
- Sandy Beach: Broad gentle strip of soft sand and shells, good for landings, camps, driftwood, and beachcombing. Waves roll in steadily, gulls wheel overhead, and the tide line collects useful flotsam. Players land boats, build driftwood fires, search for wreckage and treasure, and watch for raiders or sea creatures emerging from the surf.
- Pebble Beach: Steep shingle shore of rounded stones that crunch underfoot, making sneaking difficult and landings noisy. Waves drag the stones with a rattling roar, and the loose footing slows heavy loads. Players find agates and driftwood, struggle to approach quietly, and find it a poor camp but a good place to spot approaching ships.
- Boulder Beach: Shoreline strewn with huge rounded rocks, slow to cross, offering cover for crabs, seals, and ambushers. Waves boom between the stones and weed makes surfaces slick. Players clamber carefully, hide behind boulders, hunt shellfish and seals, and sometimes find caves or wrecks wedged deep between the great stones.
- Black Sand Beach: Dark volcanic shoreline of fine basalt grains, stark against surf and often near active vents. The sand burns underfoot in the midday sun and may hide glassy shards or steaming springs. Players gather volcanic glass and minerals, watch for lava or tremors, and may find cults or forges drawn to the nearby fire mountain.
- Rocky Shore: Wave-battered ledges and crags with slippery weed, gull colonies, and tide pools, hazardous for ships. Spray soaks everything, and unseen reefs lurk just offshore. Players collect shellfish and eggs, climb carefully to avoid falls, and salvage wrecks that the sea has thrown against the unforgiving stone.
- Sea Cliffs: Sheer wave-cut walls of rock with nesting seabirds, crashing surf below, and hard-to-reach ledges. Wind howls along the face, and loose rock threatens climbers. Players harvest eggs, scale for hidden caves or shrines, and set lighthouses or watchtowers on top, while ships below avoid the deadly rocks.
- Sea Stack: Isolated rock tower left standing offshore by erosion, favored by seabirds and hermits or lookouts. Waves hammer its base and climbing it is dangerous. Players may reach its flat summit by rope or boat to find a hermit, a nest of rare eggs, a signal beacon, or a hidden stash of smugglers.
- Sea Arch: Natural rock bridge carved by waves, framing the horizon and offering a risky passage for boats. Swells surge through the opening, and the stone above may crumble over time. Players sail beneath it as a shortcut or ritual, use it as a landmark on maps, and meet ambushers waiting in its shadow.
- Sea Cave: Wave-hollowed grotto that floods with the tide, hiding smugglers, treasure, and sudden drowning hazards. Echoes boom in the dark, and the water level rises quickly. Players explore at low tide for stashed goods or hidden passages, and must time their escape carefully before the sea seals the entrance.
- Tidal Flat: Wide expanse of mud and sand exposed at low tide, rich in shellfish, wading birds, and quicksand. The sea retreats for miles and returns swiftly, trapping the careless. Players dig for clams, track birds, and race the incoming tide, while rotting wrecks and hidden channels reward patient exploration and punish haste.
- Tide Pool: Rocky basin refilled by waves, teeming with starfish, anemones, small crabs, and slippery footing. Each pool is a tiny ecosystem, bright with color and swaying weed. Players gather bait, alchemical ingredients, and food, but a stinging anemone or a sudden wave can turn a quiet search into trouble.
- Lagoon: Calm shallow bay cut off from open sea by a reef or sandbar, sheltered and teeming with life. The water is clear, warm, and quiet, and fish dart over seagrass beds. Players anchor boats safely, fish and dive for pearls, and build villages, though sharks and storms may breach the barrier.
- Barrier Island: Long narrow sandy strip shielding the mainland from storms, with dunes, marsh, and shifting inlets. Hurricanes can reshape it overnight, and settlements there live under constant threat. Players build lighthouses or fishing huts, hunt wrecks washed ashore, and use the hidden inlets for smuggling or sheltered landings behind the dunes.
- Spit: Slender finger of sand or gravel stretching into water, useful as a natural causeway or lighthouse site. It shifts with currents and storms, and may vanish at high tide. Players use it to reach islands on foot, watch for ships from its tip, and defend or blockade the narrow approach.
- Sandbar: Submerged or exposed ridge of sand near shore that grounds boats and appears and vanishes with tides. Hidden just under the waves, it catches careless keels and lures wading travelers into rising water. Players use it as a shortcut or stepping stone, strand pursuers upon it, and sometimes find wrecks snagged along its length.
- Estuary: Brackish meeting point of river and sea, rich in fish and birds, ideal for harbors and trade. Tides push salt water upstream, and mud flats and marshes line the broad channel. Players dock at busy ports, fish and trade, and navigate carefully past shifting sandbars while pirates and tax collectors watch for prey.
- Fjord: Deep steep-walled glacial inlet with still dark water, waterfalls, and isolated settlements under towering cliffs. Echoes carry far, and fierce winds funnel down the narrow channel. Players sail between sheer walls to reach hidden villages, hunt for whales or seals, and may encounter raiders or sea creatures lurking in the black depths.
- Bay: Broad curving inlet sheltered from open sea, a natural harbor for ports, fishing fleets, and naval bases. Calm water and gentle shores invite settlement, trade, and shipbuilding. Players dock, resupply, and recruit crews, and may find themselves caught in blockades, harbor politics, or sudden storms that sweep in across the open mouth.
- Cove: Small sheltered inlet ringed by cliffs, perfect for smugglers, hidden landings, and secret anchorages. A narrow entrance and high walls conceal ships from the open sea. Players stash contraband, hide from pursuers, or stumble onto pirate camps, while tides and hidden rocks make entry risky for those without local knowledge.
- Headland: Rocky promontory jutting into the sea, ideal for lighthouses, watchtowers, and fortresses guarding approaches. Wind and surf batter the exposed point, and the view stretches for miles. Players man the beacon, defend the strategic position, or climb to spot approaching fleets, and wrecks on the surrounding rocks tell grim stories.
- Skerries: Scattering of tiny rocky islets and reefs that wreck unwary ships yet shelter seabirds and fishers. Narrow, winding channels weave between low stones, and fog makes navigation treacherous. Players learn secret routes, shelter from storms, collect eggs and seals, and may find smuggler caches or the bones of unlucky ships.
- Archipelago: Cluster of many islands with winding channels, ideal for island hopping, pirate lairs, and scattered communities. Each island has its own character, from jungle to bare rock, and sea lanes twist among them. Players trade, raid, and explore, using hidden coves for ambushes while mapping safe routes through uncertain waters.
- Atoll: Ring-shaped coral island enclosing a calm lagoon, with palms, white sand, and a narrow pass. The outer reef breaks heavy surf while the inner water stays gentle and clear. Players shelter ships inside, dive for pearls and wrecks, and defend the single channel, which makes it a natural fortress or refuge.
- Coral Island: Low sandy isle built on a reef, ringed by shallow turquoise water and teeming marine life. Palms and scrub cover the high ground, and sharp coral threatens bare feet and hulls. Players fish, gather coconuts and pearls, and risk storms and tides that can drown the island's low and fragile ground.
- Dune Coast: Shifting sandy ridges backing the beach, held by grasses, home to hidden hollows and wandering sands. Wind reshapes the hills constantly, burying paths and uncovering old things. Players find sheltered hollows for camps, dig up lost wrecks or ruins, and risk disorientation or ambush among the identical, rolling crests.
### Open Sea
- Open Sea: Endless rolling water with no landmarks stretches to every horizon, demanding careful navigation, rationed supplies, and a good sense of changing weather. Sea monsters and rare passing sails break the loneliness. Players chart courses by stars and dead reckoning, hunting for islands before provisions and patience run out.
- Ocean Gyre: A huge, slow-spinning system of currents drags drifting ships into its calm center, where floating debris, plastic-like flotsam, and strange wildlife collect. Escaping takes patience or clever sailing. Players might salvage junk islands, meet stranded castaways, or find derelicts circling for decades.
- Whirlpool: A swirling vortex of churning water drags small ships downward, usually forming in dangerous narrows or tidal channels. It tests the nerve and skill of any helmsman, and the roar warns of its presence. Players must time passages carefully, or deliberately ride it toward sunken treasures below.
- Maelstrom: A colossal, violent whirlpool of legend spirals down to unknown depths, its thunderous roar carrying for miles and devouring whole fleets without mercy. Wreckage rains around its rim. Players might seek it as a gateway to a hidden undersea realm, or flee from its pull.
- Sargasso Sea: A calm expanse of tangled drifting seaweed stalls sailing ships in windless water, sheltering crabs, eels, and the ghostly hulks of long-lost wrecks caught in its mats. Slow starvation and eerie quiet threaten crews. Players cut free, scavenge old cargo, and uncover forgotten ship mysteries.
- Ice Edge Sea: Cold gray water choked with drifting floes and looming bergs sits at the frozen margin of the polar world. Whales, seals, and seabirds abound, but hulls can be crushed in the shifting ice. Players hunt, navigate leads between floes, and risk freezing winds and sudden storms.
- Rogue Wave Zone: A stretch of ocean where sudden towering waves rise from seemingly nowhere, smashing even sturdy vessels without warning in otherwise ordinary seas. Survivors tell of walls of water and troughs deep as canyons. Players must brace, steer into the swell, and recover wreckage afterward.
- Phosphorescent Sea: Water that glows at night with living blue-green light, outlining every wake, oar stroke, and fish in shimmering trails. Its beauty is eerie and calming, but it makes stealth nearly impossible. Players can use the glow to track enemies or lure creatures, and sneaking past patrols becomes harder.
- Dead Calm: A windless, glassy ocean beneath a merciless sun strands sailing ships for days or weeks, draining water, supplies, and morale as the horizon never changes. Tempers flare and rumors of curses spread. Players must row, conjure wind, ration carefully, or face mutiny and thirst.
- Storm Sea: Lightning-lit waters of howling wind, driving rain, and towering swells toss ships, snap masts, and wash sailors overboard. The sky turns black and compasses spin. Players fight to keep their vessel afloat, lash down cargo, and may be flung toward uncharted shores or into monster-haunted wrecks.
### Reefs & Shallow Life
- Coral Reef: A colorful living structure of branching and brain corals bursts with reef fish, sharks, rays, and tight winding passages beneath sunlit shallows. Sharp coral can slice skin and rip hulls. Players dive for pearls and food, explore hidden grottoes, and avoid predators lurking in the crevices.
- Barrier Reef: A long offshore wall of coral shields a calm lagoon from ocean surf, making a navigational hazard with only a few narrow gaps for ships to pass. Waves crash white along its length. Players seek the safe channels, shelter inside the lagoon, or smuggle goods through the gaps.
- Fringing Reef: A coral shelf hugs the shoreline, easy to wade and snorkel yet sharp underfoot, with tidal pools full of urchins, crabs, small fish, and bright anemones. Low tide reveals its maze of channels. Players gather shellfish, hunt for tools and treasure, and risk cuts, stings, and rising tides.
- Kelp Forest: Towering, swaying groves of giant seaweed rise from the seabed toward the surface, filtering dim green light and sheltering otters, fish, and ambush predators among the fronds. Divers can tangle easily in the strands. Players harvest kelp, hunt, and sneak through living curtains of cover.
- Seagrass Meadow: A shallow underwater prairie of waving green blades is grazed by turtles and manatees and serves as a nursery for countless young fish. It is peaceful and bright but silty, hiding rays and crabs. Players graze, fish, and track migrations, or protect the meadow from damage.
